A linear bound for the size of the finite terminal assembly of a directed non-cooperative tile assembly system
Sergiu Ivanov, Damien Regnault

Abstract
Introduced in [ 32 ] , the abstract tile assembly model (aTAM) is a model of DNA self-assembly. Most of the studies focus on cooperative aTAM where a form of synchronization between the tiles is possible. Simulating Turing machines is achievable in this context. Few results and constructions are known for the non-cooperative case (a variant of Wang tilings [ 30 ] where assemblies do not need to cover the whole plane and some mismatches may occur). For example, assembly of a square of width n n is done with 2 ​ n − 1 2n-1 tiles types whereas only Θ ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( n ) log ⁡ ( log ⁡ ( n ) ) ) \Theta(\frac{\log(n)}{\log(\log(n))}) are required for the cooperative case [ 1 ] . Introduced by P.-É. Meunier in [ 22 ] , efficient paths are a non-trivial construction for non-cooperative aTAM designed with n n different tile types and reaching a distance linearly greater than n n . Improved in [ 19 ] , efficient paths were shown to be able to reach a distance of n ​ log ⁡ ( n ) n\log(n) . Assembling them relies heavily on a form of “non-determinism”. Indeed, the set of tiles may produce different finite terminal assemblies but they all contain the same efficient path. In this paper, we prove that this non-determinism is strictly necessary for assembling the efficient paths of [ 19 ] . More formally, we show that if the terminal assembly of a directed non-cooperative tile assembly system (a model where only one terminal assembly is produced) is finite then its width and length are linear in the number of tiles. This result also implies that the construction of a square of width n n using 2 ​ n − 1 2n-1 tiles types is asymptotically optimal. Moreover, we hope that the techniques introduced here will lead to a better comprehension of the non-directed case.



1
Introduction

Modern processors align several hundred of billions of transistors on small chips. Independently, each transistor only simulates a simple Boolean function, but together they can achieve far more complex computations.
Developing a formalism to describe complex mathematical functions by iterating simple building blocks originates from the seminal work by Turing on the abstract Turing machine—the very first formal model of computing. Studying both this abstract model and concrete computers derived from it became what is commonly known as computer science.

More recently, the DNA molecule emerged as a possible elementary component to carry computation instead of the conventional transistor. Indeed, Adleman
[
2
]
showed that algorithms can be implemented by mixing together cleverly designed DNA strands, creating a massively parallel model of computing. Several approaches relying on self-assembly were later implemented: in
[
27
]
Rothemund developed a technique of
DNA origami
to fold a long DNA strand (scaffold) using hundreds of shorter DNA strands (staples), resulting in specific shapes of size on the order of hundreds of nanometers. In
[
28
]
fractals were assembled out of DNA. In
[
25
]
artificial neural networks were simulated. In
[
35
]
cyclic machines were experimentally implemented, using DNA both as machine material and as fuel.

Over time, several possible applications have emerged. On the one hand, DNA can be an efficient way to store large amounts of information for millions of years
[
5
]
. On the other hand, DNA can be used to construct chemical seeds allowing to detect small concentrations of a target molecular compound
[
23
]
. In
[
31
]
a DNA cage was built around the hepatitis B virus to neutralize it in vitro. The
crisscross slats
approach in
[
33
]
allows the assembly of large structures several microns in size, opening up the possibility of interaction with biological cells.

Abstractly, some approaches to computing with DNA can be seen as a population of tiles assembling into the target structure by attaching to a seed, which represents the input. The corresponding theoretical model of computation called the
abstract Tile Assembly Model
(aTAM) was introduced by Winfree in
[
26
,
32
]
. The aTAM relies on square tiles with glues on each of the four sides. When the glues on two tiles match, the tiles bind together forming an assembly. This assembly grows until it becomes terminal,
i.e.
until no more tiles can bind to it. This model can be implemented experimentally by representing tiles as small DNA complexes binding to a seed (a DNA origami) to carry out the computation, as shown in
[
34
]
. Furthermore, aTAM is able to simulate crisscross slats
[
9
]
. Lots of variants of aTAM exist, and notably cooperative aTAM (or temperature
2
aTAM) including a mechanism by which different parts of the assembly may synchronize. In this case, simulating a Turing machine is easily achieved and cooperative aTAM was shown to be intrinsically universal
[
7
]
. Due to the possible applications to DNA computing, studies also focus on the shapes which can be assembled. For example,
[
29
]
focuses on the Kolmogorov complexity of the shapes and shows a strong connection between the complexity of a shape and the number of distinct tile types necessary to assemble it. The special case of the square was studied in deep detail in
[
1
]
, this shape being a kind of benchmark for computational expressiveness.

1.1
Non-cooperative aTAM

From this point on, we focus on non-cooperative aTAM. In this variant, there is no mechanism to synchronise different parts of the assembly beyond concurrency: the first path to grow blocks and prevents the growth of others. Non-cooperative aTAM can be seen as a variant of Wang tiles
[
30
]
where the computation starts from a seed and mismatches are allowed. Surprisingly, this model proved very hard to study, and very few results were known for a long time. Concerning decidability seen as the limit of what can be achieved, slight modifications are enough to simulate the Turing machine. In
[
6
]
simulation of Turing machines was achieved in
3D
and was done almost surely by introducing probabilities. It was also achieved by introducing negative glue weights
[
24
]
, by considering polyomino tiles
[
10
]
, polygonal tiles
[
13
]
, detachable glues
[
14
]
, or by performing the assembly in several steps
[
4
]
. Nevertheless, the question is still open for non-cooperative aTAM. Several recent studies hint that it is likely decidable: non-cooperative aTAM is not intrinsically universal
[
16
,
21
]
, it is not able to simulate the Turing machine in the classical way
[
21
]
, and a
2D
pumping lemma was published in
[
17
]
. A weaker version of non-cooperative aTAM called
directed
where only one final terminal assembly is allowed was shown to be decidable by combining
[
8
]
and
[
17
]
.

Although all these results point towards a negative answer, if non-cooperative aTAM were undecidable, it would be a surprising breakthrough and could lead to surprising results. It is worth remembering that such a scenario had already occurred for Wang tiles: the domino problem was initially conjectured to be undecidable, and proving this conjecture wrong required exhibiting aperiodic tilings. In this context, aperiodicity is an unusual way of initializing computation. Subsequently, aperiodic tilings were extensively studied and became a model of crystallography.

Concerning the complexity of non-cooperative aTAM, assembling rectangles and squares in
3D
was achieved in
[
12
,
11
]
, and the upper and lower bounds on the number of tile types almost match. In
2D
, lower bounds are known for the case in which mismatches are disallowed
[
15
]
. Concerning the main
2D
model, the existence of efficient paths of size
n\log n
was shown in
[
19
]
(improving on a previous construction in
[
22
]
). An efficient path appears in every terminal assembly and only requires a tile set of size
n
, whereas an auxiliary path may appear somewhere else in the terminal assembly. A limitation of this approach was also obtained in
[
17
]
: it is impossible to construct efficient paths of arbitrary lengths.
Concerning the directed non-cooperative aTAM, the work
[
20
]
—an update of
[
8
]
—shows that only four kinds of terminal assemblies exist, one of them being a bi-periodic grid which must be hardcoded in the tile set.

1.2
Our contribution

In this paper, we consider another one of this four kinds of assemblies: the finite terminal assembly. Our main result is a linear bound (in the number of tile types) on the size of finite terminal assemblies. Besides advancing the understanding of directed non-cooperative aTAM, this result has several major implications. Firstly, it partially solves a conjecture stated in
[
26
,
32
]
about the asymptotic bound on assembling a square which is now proven to be linear. Note that finding the exact multiplicative constant is still an open question. Secondly, it is another hint that directed non-cooperative aTAM is not able to perform any complex computation. Thirdly, our result highlights a fundamental difference with non-cooperative aTAM: concurrency is required to assemble the efficient paths exhibited in
[
19
]
. Fourthly, our bound opens new perspectives for improving several previous results such as the pumping lemma in
[
17
]
. Indeed, multiple major results rely on understanding the “information flow” in an assembly, like for example the window movie lemma stated in
[
16
]
. Intuitively, a window delimits an area of the plane and a window movie is the order of placement, position, and glue types appearing along the window. The main theorem in
[
17
]
was obtained by focusing on a particular window movie and two of its critical glues. In this paper, we improve on the previous reasoning by showing that some information flowing through the window cannot be new and is therefore redundant. A similar approach has also recently been used to show that some fractals cannot be assembled in the cooperative case
[
3
]
. Using such improved tools may have consequences for other model and results.

This paper is structured as follows.
Section
2
introduces the definitions. Due to the length of the paper, we present in Section
3
the key ideas arranged in a roadmap, and explain how they articulate in the proof of the main result. The four following sections implement these ideas, solving many technical problems which commonly appear when studying temperature
1
. Sections
4
,
5
, and
6
are rather independent. They start by a presentation of the new tools, then the related results are shown, and each section is concluded by explaining the place of the presented tools in the main proof. The last Section
7
puts all arguments together thereby providing the definitive proof.

2
Abstract tile assembly model

As usual, let
\mathbb{R}
be the set of real numbers, let
\mathbb{Z}
be the set of all integers, let
\mathbb{N}
be the set of all natural numbers including 0, and let
\mathbb{N}^{*}
be the set of all natural numbers excluding 0. The domain of a function
f
is denoted
dom(f)
, and its range (or image) is denoted
f(dom(f))
.

2.1
General definitions and main theorem

A
tile type
is a unit square with four sides,
each consisting of a glue
type
and a nonnegative integer
strength
. Let
T
be a finite set of tile types.
The sides of a tile type are respectively called north, east, south, and west.

An
assembly
is a partial function
\alpha:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\dashrightarrow T
where
T
is a set of tile types and the domain of
\alpha
(denoted
dom(\alpha)
) is connected.
1
1

1

Intuitively, an assembly is a positioning of unit-sized tiles, each from some set of tile types
T
, so that their centers are placed on (some of) the elements of the discrete plane
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and such that those elements of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
form a connected set of points.

Two tile types in an assembly are said to
bind
(or
interact
, or to be

stably attached
) if the glue types on their abutting sides are
equal, and have strength
\geq 1
. An assembly
\alpha
induces an undirected
weighted
binding graph

G_{\alpha}=(V,E)
, where
V=dom(\alpha)
, and
there is an edge
\{a,b\}\in E
if and only if the tiles at positions
a
and
b
interact, and
this edge is weighted by the glue strength of that interaction. The
assembly is said to be
\tau
-stable if every cut of
G_{\alpha}
has weight at
least
\tau
.

Given two
\tau
-stable assemblies
\alpha
and
\beta
, the union of
\alpha
and
\beta
, written
\alpha\cup\beta
, is an assembly defined if and only if and for all
p\in dom(\alpha)\cap dom(\beta)
,
\alpha(p)=\beta(p)
and either at least one tile of
\alpha
binds with a tile of
\beta
or
dom(\alpha)\cap dom(\beta)\neq\emptyset
. Then, for all
p\in dom(\alpha)
, we have
(\alpha\cup\beta)(p)=\alpha(p)
and for all
p\in dom(\beta)
, we have
(\alpha\cup\beta)(p)=\beta(p)
.

A
tile assembly system
is a triple
\mathcal{T}=(T,\sigma,\tau)
,
where
T
is a finite set of tile types,
\sigma
is a
\tau
-stable assembly called the
seed
, and

\tau\in\mathbb{N}
is the
temperature
.
Throughout this article,
\tau=1
.

Given two
\tau
-stable assemblies
\alpha
and
\beta
, we say that
\alpha
is a

subassembly
of
\beta
, and write
\alpha\sqsubseteq\beta
, if

dom(\alpha)\subseteq dom(\beta)
and for all
p\in dom(\alpha)
,

\alpha(p)=\beta(p)
.
We also write

\alpha\rightarrow_{1}^{\mathcal{T}}\beta
if we can obtain
\beta
from

\alpha
by the binding of a single tile type, that is:
\alpha\sqsubseteq\beta
,
|dom(\beta)\setminus dom(\alpha)|=1
and the tile type at the position
dom(\beta)\setminus dom(\alpha)
binds to
\alpha
at that position. We say that
\gamma
is

producible
from
\alpha
if there is a (possibly empty)
sequence
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}
where
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
,
\alpha=\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{n}=\gamma
, such that

\alpha_{1}\rightarrow_{1}^{\mathcal{T}}\alpha_{2}\rightarrow_{1}^{\mathcal{T}}\ldots\rightarrow_{1}^{\mathcal{T}}\alpha_{n}
.

The set of
productions
, or
producible assemblies
, of a tile assembly system
\mathcal{T}=(T,\sigma,\tau)
is the set of all assemblies producible
from the seed assembly
\sigma
and is written
\mathcal{A}[{\mathcal{T}}]
. An assembly
\alpha
is called
terminal
if there is no
\beta
such that
\alpha\rightarrow_{1}^{\mathcal{T}}\beta
. The set of all terminal assemblies of
\mathcal{T}
is denoted
\mathcal{A}_{\Box}[{\mathcal{T}}]
.
If there is a unique terminal assembly,
i.e.

|\mathcal{A}_{\Box}[{\mathcal{T}}]|=1
, then
\mathcal{T}
is
directed
and its unique terminal assembly is called
\gamma
along this paper.

The easternmost column of a finite assembly
\alpha
is designated by
e_{\alpha}=\max\{x:\exists y,(x,y)\in dom\{\alpha\}\}
,
w_{\alpha}
,
n_{\alpha}
,
s_{\alpha}
are defined similarly for the westernmost, northernmost and southernmost columns of the assembly respectively. The
horizontal width
of an assembly
\alpha
is
e_{\alpha}-w_{\alpha}
and its
vertical height

n_{\alpha}-s_{\alpha}
. The following theorem is the main contribution of this paper.

Theorem 1
.

Consider a directed tile assembly system
(T,\sigma,1)
, if its terminal assembly
\gamma
is finite then its horizontal width and vertical height are bounded by
7|\sigma|+58|T|+30
.

For the rest of the article, we consider a directed tile assembly system
\mathcal{T}=(T,\sigma,1)
and we suppose that its terminal assembly
\gamma
is finite. Also, we assume that the assembly grows the furthest away from the seed to the east,
i.e.
:

e_{\alpha}-e_{\sigma}=\max\{e_{\alpha}-e_{\sigma},n_{\alpha}-n_{\sigma},|w_{\alpha}-w_{\sigma}|,|s_{\alpha}-s_{\sigma}|\}.

With this assumption it is sufficient to bound
e_{\alpha}-e_{\sigma}
by
3|\sigma|+29|T|+15
to prove the main theorem. The proof of this result is constructive: we design an algorithm which takes as input an assembly
\gamma
such that
e_{\gamma}>3|\sigma|+29|T|+15
and it will either:

•

output a certificate that the tile assembly system is not directed;

•

bind a new tile to the assembly, showing that
\gamma
is not terminal.

By iterating this algorithm on a large enough finite assembly, we are able to make it grow arbitrarily large. Note that, by using the pumping lemma of
[
17
]
, an infinite periodic path can be built when the assembly is large enough. We do not explicitly write the algorithm in this paper, but it can be directly derived from the constructive proofs, which are concluded by exhibiting a contradiction: either
\gamma
is not terminal, or the tile assembly system is not directed.

3
State of the art, roadmap and main obstacles

Our proof starts by following the same steps as the ones done in
[
17
]
. In Subsections
3.1
,
3.2
,
3.3
and
3.4
, we present the reasoning and the tools necessary to construct a specific assembly called the
shield
. Our proof then diverges from previous approaches, as shields were complex to assemble, despite being useful in proving the pumping lemma in
[
17
]
. Nevertheless, in Subsection
3.5
, we show that shields take a simple form when the terminal assembly of a directed tile assembly system is finite. In Subsection
3.6
, we explain the key idea on how to use shields in an original way to obtain the main result of this paper. Note that this section is only a roadmap: we identify several technical difficulties, called
locks
, which are solved in the following sections.

3.1
Paths

Since there is no possible synchronization between the tiles in the non-cooperative case, we focus on
paths
instead of assemblies.
Formally, a path
P
is a finite sequence of tiles
(P_{i})_{0\leq i\leq|P|-1}
such that each tile
P_{i}
interacts with the next one
P_{i+1}
of the sequence. Moreover, a path does not intersect with the seed and is simple: two different tiles of
P
do not occupy the same position. The length of
P
is the size
|P|-1
of the sequence. If the first tile of the path interacts with a tile of the seed
\sigma
then the path is
producible
. The last tile of a producible path is the
easternmost one
if and only if it is the only tile of
P
on column
e_{P}
with
e_{P}>e_{\sigma}
; moreover, a producible path is
extremal
if
e_{P}=e_{\gamma}
(we remind that
\gamma
is the unique terminal assembly). We say that a path
P
is a
subassembly of
\gamma
if and only if each tile of
P
belongs to
\gamma
. Of course, producible and extremal paths are subassemblies of
\gamma
.

We now introduce some basic operations on paths. The
concatenation
of a finite path
P
with a path
Q
is the concatenation
PQ
of these two paths as sequences.
PQ
is a path if and only if (1) the last tile of
P
interacts with the first tile of
Q
and (2)
P
and
Q
do not intersect. For a path
P=P_{0}\ldots P_{i}P_{i+1}\ldots P_{j}\ldots
, we define the
subpath
of P between indices
i
and
j
, both included, in the following way:
P_{i,i+1,\ldots,j}=P_{i}P_{i+1}\ldots P_{j}
.
In the special case of a subpath where
i=0
(resp.
j=|P|-1
), we say that
P_{0,1,\ldots,j}
(resp.
P_{j,\ldots,|P|-1}
) is a
prefix
(resp.
suffix
) of
P
. Consider a vector
\overrightarrow{v}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and a tile
A
, the translation of
A
by
\overrightarrow{v}
is the tile
A+\overrightarrow{v}=(\mathrm{pos}(A)+\overrightarrow{v},\mathrm{type}(A))
. The translation of a path
P
by
\overrightarrow{v}
is
P+\overrightarrow{v}=(P_{0}+\overrightarrow{v})(P_{1}+\overrightarrow{v})\ldots(P_{|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{v})
. Consider two indices
0\leq i\leq j\leq|P|-1
, by abuse of notation we designate by
\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
the vector
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{pos}(P_{i})\mathrm{pos}(P_{j})}
. Also, we denote by
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{j})
the glue used to bind tile
P_{i}
with
P_{i+1}
. It is situated on a
glue column

c
between the two tiles (and thus between two columns
c-0.5<c<c+0.5
). The path
\text{rev}({P})
designates the path
P
in reverse: for all
0\leq i\leq|P|-1
, we have
\text{rev}({P})_{i}=P_{|P|-1-i}
. The assembly
\mathrm{asm}{(P)}
is the representation of the path
P
as an assembly (the order of the tiles is not taken into account anymore). This notation allows to merge two paths
P
and
Q
as
\mathrm{asm}{(P)}\cup\mathrm{asm}{(Q)}
. This operation can be done even if
P
and
Q
intersect and thus is more general than the concatenation.

\mathcal{B}_{0}=w_{\sigma}-2|T|-1
=e_{\sigma+|T|+1.5}
\mathcal{B}_{1}
\mathcal{B}_{2}
=e_{\sigma+5|T|+1.5}
\mathcal{B}_{3}
\mathcal{B}_{4}
7|\sigma|+58|T|+30=\mathcal{B}_{5}

Figure 1:
An extremal path
P
and the different bounds used during the analysis. Along the different figures, the seed is always shown in black tiles. This path
P
crosses column
\mathcal{B}_{5}
, leading to the contradiction implying that the terminal assembly is in fact infinite.

The previous results of
[
17
]
and the ones of this paper are obtained by showing that the farther away a path grows from the seed, the more it is constrained. Figure
1
shows an extremal path and several bounds. The main one
\mathcal{B}_{5}
is the bound used to prove the main Theorem
1
: if a path crosses this column then the terminal assembly is infinite. The bound
\mathcal{B}_{0}
shows that an extremal path cannot grow too far to the west of the seed, i.e. on the opposite side of the seed from its last tile.

Lock 1
.

If
P
is an extremal path, then
w_{P}\geq\mathcal{B}_{0}=w_{\sigma}-2|T|-1
.

This result was also proven in
[
18
]
, in a slightly different context.
We give a simplified proof of this result in Section
5
.

The path
P
will be cut in three parts. The first one is its beginning (in dark green in Figure
1
) which is too close to the seed to be useful
2
2

2

That is because the
visible glues
between the columns
B_{0}
and
B_{1}
will point east. The notion of a visible glue is formally introduced in the next subsection.
. The middle of the path
P
(in green in Figure
1
) will start on a column with index at least
\mathcal{B}_{1}=e_{\sigma+|T|+1.5}
and will end on a column with index at most
\mathcal{B}_{2}=e_{\sigma+5|T|+1.5}
(the exact beginning and ending of this subpath of
P
are explained in Figure
2
and Subsection
3.4
). In this subpath of
P
, we aim to locate two indices
0\leq i\leq j\leq|P|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{j}P_{j+1})
have the same type, allowing us to try to assemble
P_{0,\ldots,i}(P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{j}P_{i}})
or
P_{0,\ldots,j}(P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}})
. Note that copying and pasting the end of
P
is not always possible due to possible intersections between the beginning of
P
and its translated suffix. Thus, one key step explained in Subsections
3.4
and
3.5
is to select
i
and
j
such that
P_{0,\ldots,i}(P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{j}P_{i}})
is always a producible path. Finally, the end of the path
P
(in pale green in Figure
1
) has to be large enough such that when it is copied and pasted, it will create a large obstacle, called a
shield
. This shield will be used to find other indices where different parts of
P
can be copied and pasted. Repeating this reasoning will eventually lead to a contradiction. The role of bounds
\mathcal{B}_{3}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
will be explained in Subsection
3.6
, we do not give here their explicit definition since their values depend on the positions of
P_{i}
and
P_{j}
.

3.2
Visible glues of an extremal path

All results of this paper rely on the notion of
visible
glues introduced in
[
21
]
. Consider an index
0\leq i\leq|P|-1
,
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
is
horizontal
if both tiles are on the same line or
vertical
if both tiles are on the same column. A horizontal glue points east if
P_{i}
is west of
P_{i+1}
otherwise it points west. The glue is visible from the south (resp. north) in
P
if and only it is the southernmost (northernmost) glue of
P
on its column and if there are no tiles of the seed on both adjacent columns. We designate by
l^{i}
the
glue ray
associated to
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
:
l^{i}
is a ray starting at the position of
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
going south (resp. north) if
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
is visible from the south (resp. north). If the last tile of a producible path
P
is the easternmost one (which is the case for an extremal path) then we have the following properties (see Figure
2
):

\mathcal{B}_{2}

i_{3}
i_{4}
i_{5}

The path
P
of Figure

) and

is an example of a glue visible from the south and pointing west. The
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i_{3}}P_{i_{3}+1})
is the westernmost glue visible from the south and pointing east. This glue is on a glue column
c=\mathcal{B}_{1}-1
in this example (by Lock
2
,
c\leq\mathcal{B}_{1}
). The last glue of
P
,
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i_{7}}P_{i_{7}+1})
, is always visible from the south and from the north. The
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i_{4}}P_{i_{4}+1})
and the

.
The limit between the dark green and green subpaths is delimited by
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i_{2}}P_{i_{2}+1})
which is the first glue of the span of
P
on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}
. The limit between the green and pale green subpaths is delimited by the last glue of
P
on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{2}
which happens to be
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i_{6}}P_{i_{6}+1})
in this simple example.

Fact 1
.

Consider a producible path
P
whose last tile is the easternmost one. Then up to some symmetries
3
3

3

Swapping the north and south glues of all tile types leads to horizontal symmetry of the producible assemblies.
, we can consider that all the glues of
P
which are visible from the north point east.

Lemma 1
.

Consider a producible path
P
whose last glue is visible from the north. Then there exists a glue column
c
such that all glues visible from the south on glue column
c^{\prime}<c
point west and all glues visible from the south on glue column
c^{\prime}\geq c
point east.

Lock 2
.

Consider a producible path
P
whose last tile is the easternmost one and
0\leq i\leq|P|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
is visible from the south on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}
. Then
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
points east.

Lemma 2
.

Consider a producible path
P
whose last glue is visible from the north and indices
0\leq i<j<|P|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{j}P_{j+1})
are both visible from the south on glue columns
c
and
c^{\prime}
respectively. If both glues points east (resp. west) then
c<c^{\prime}
(resp
c>c^{\prime}
).

If
P
is an extremal path, then these three lemmas and Lock
2
imply that all glues which are visible from the north or south in
P
on a glue column
c\geq\mathcal{B}_{1}
point east. The proofs of these lemmas are, now, classic results available in
[
17
,
21
]
and will not be proven here again. The proof of Lock
2
is available in
[
17
]
and relies on a complex intermediate lemma that will be simplified in Section
5
.

3.3
Cut, span and workspace

Combining two visible glues of a producible path
P
allows us to delimit a part of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
which is an entire vertical half-plane, plus some other parts depending on the exact shape of
P
between the two visibles glues. When the visible glues are chosen appropriately, this area does not contain any tile of
\sigma
or any tile of
P
before the first visible glue. Then, we are free to edit
P
in this area without fearing a conflict with one of its previous parts or the seed. More formally, consider two indices
0\leq s\leq|P|-2
and
0\leq n\leq|P|-2
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{s}P_{s+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{n}P_{n+1})
are visible from the south and from the north respectively in
P
. If
s\leq n
(resp.
n\leq s
),
(s,n)
(resp.
(n,s)
) is called an
upward (resp. downward) cut
of
P
because the bi-infinite curve made of
l^{s}
,
P_{s,\ldots,n+1}
and
l^{n}
cuts the space in two areas, see Figure
3
. If
\mathrm{glue}(P_{s}P_{s+1})
(resp.
\mathrm{glue}(P_{n}P_{n+1})
) points east then the seed and the beginning of
P
are in the west side of the cut and cannot block the growth of new paths in the east side of the cut. The east side of the cut is called the
workspace
of the cut
(s,n)
(resp.
(n,s)
) of
P
, denoted by
\mathcal{C}
:

Fact 2
.

Consider a producible path
P
, a cut
(s,n)
of
P
and a path
Q
such that
Q_{0}
interacts with
P_{s}
and
Q
is in the workspace
\mathcal{C}
of the cut
(s,n)
of
P
4
4

4

Note that tiles are placed on the discrete plane
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
but a workspace is a part of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For a path
Q
, this implies that
Q
is in
\mathcal{C}
if and only if the tiles of
Q
are in the area but also the segments linking the different tiles (including the glues).
. Then
P_{0,\ldots,s}Q
is producible.

),

Along the paper, we will often consider the special case where the two visible glues
s
and
n
are on the same glue column. In this case, the cut is called the
span
of
P
on glue column
c
. The type of a span is the type of
\mathrm{glue}(P_{s}P_{s+1})
(resp.
\mathrm{glue}(P_{n}P_{n+1})
), it points in the same direction as
\mathrm{glue}(P_{s}P_{s+1})
(resp.
\mathrm{glue}(P_{n}P_{n+1})
), and its width is
y_{P_{n}}-y_{P_{s}}
.

3.4
Classical reasoning, right-priority and shield

The result of
[
17
]
is obtained by combining two spans of the same type and direction. Indeed, consider an extremal path
P
and two glue columns
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c<c^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
such that the spans
(s,n)
and
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
of
P
on glue columns
c
and
c^{\prime}
respectively have the same characteristics (they are both upward or downward spans, point east and have the same type). Then we can try to assemble
P_{0,\ldots,s}(P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s}^{\prime}})
or
P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}}(P_{s+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s}^{\prime}})
. To explain what may occur when trying to assemble such paths, we use the extremal path
P
of Figure
4
(a) as an example.

Figure 4:
(a) The upward spans
(s,n)
and
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
of a path
P
. The workspace
\mathcal{D}
of span
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
is in yellow. The beginning
P_{0,\ldots,s}
of the path is in green, the middle
P_{s+1,\ldots,s^{\prime}}
of the path is in yellow and the end
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
of the path is in blue. (b) We try to assemble
Q=P_{s,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
at the end of
P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}}
for as long as possible. The tiles of
Q
are in orange and magenta, the orange ones are common to
Q
and
P
. (c) Since the tile assembly system is directed,
Q
can merge with
P
. (d) We try to assemble
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
at the end of
P_{0,\ldots,s}
for as long as possible. The workspace
\mathcal{C}
of the span
(s,n)
is in blue.

In Figure
4
(a), the spans
(s,n)
and
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
are upward spans and
\mathcal{D}
is the workspace of
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
. In Figure
4
(b), we try to assemble the path
Q=P_{s+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s}^{\prime}}
at the end of
P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}}
. While doing so, the following important events occur in this example. Firstly, there exists an index
0\leq i\leq|Q|-1
such that
Q_{0,\ldots,i}
grows along
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
by putting its tiles in the same positions,
i.e.
for all
0\leq t\leq i
, we have
\mathrm{pos}(Q_{t})=\mathrm{pos}(P_{s^{\prime}+1+t})
(the tiles in orange in Figure
4
(b)). Secondly,
Q
turns right of
P_{s^{\prime},\ldots,|P|-1}
at
\mathrm{pos}(Q_{i})
(for a formal definition of this notion see
[
17
,
21
]
) and stays inside
\mathcal{D}
until it intersects with
P
at
\mathrm{pos}(P_{k})=\mathrm{pos}(Q_{j})
. Thirdly,
Q
leaves
\mathcal{D}
after
Q_{j}
,
i.e.
no tiles of
Q_{j+1,\ldots,|Q|-1}
are inside
\mathcal{D}
. Fourthly,
Q
intersects with
P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}}
. Thus
P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}}Q
is not self-avoiding and is not a path but
P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}}Q_{0,\ldots,j}
is a path and it is producible by Fact
2
. Moreover, since the tile assembly system is directed then we have
P_{k}=Q_{j}
and
Q_{0,\ldots,i}=P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,s^{\prime}+1+i}
is the largest common prefix of
Q
and
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
.

Now, we focus on the fact that
Q
turns right of
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
. This event implies that
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}
turns left of
P_{s,\ldots,|P|-1}
, see Figure
4
(d). By doing so,
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}
immediately leaves the workspace
\mathcal{C}
of the span
(s,n)
of
P
. In this example,
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}
quickly collides with
P_{0,\ldots,s}
. Then, in the general case we have three possibilities:

Fact 3
.

Consider an extremal path
P
and two glue columns
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c<c^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
such that the spans
(s,n)
and
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
of
P
on glue columns
c
and
c^{\prime}
respectively have the same characteristics. Then one of the following holds:

1.

P_{s}(P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}})
turns right of
P_{s,\ldots,n+1}
;

2.

or
P_{s^{\prime}}(P_{s+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}})
turns right of
P_{s^{\prime},\ldots,|P|-1}
;

3.

or the width of span
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
is less or equal to the width of span
(s,n)
.

When the first case of Fact
3
occurs, a suffix of
(P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}})
will be used as a shield.
For the third case of Fact
3
, it was proven in
[
17
]
that in this case the path
P
is pumpable or fragile which means that either the tile assembly system is not directed or that the terminal assembly is infinite. These events cannot occur in our setting. We do not explain this argument in the roadmap.

In Subsection
3.5
, we will consider a specific setting where the second case of Fact
3
also leads to a contradiction. To do so, consider the tile
P_{k}=Q_{j}
of Figure
4
: it is the only intersection between
Q_{i+1,\ldots,|Q|-1}
and
P_{s^{\prime}+1+i,\ldots,|P|-1}
in this simple example. Since the tile assembly system is directed, it is possible to “merge” these two paths together. Indeed,
R=P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}}Q_{0,\ldots,j}P_{k+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
is a producible extremal path, see Figure
4
(c). In
[
17
]
, this operation is repeated several times to build a path called a
shield
. From this point on, our reasoning differs from
[
17
]
and we introduce a setting where shields take a very simple form. Indeed,
P
and
R
are both extremal paths. Moreover, they share the common prefix
P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}+1+i}
. Nevertheless the path
R
turns right of
P
at tile
P_{i}
. Then, we say that
R
is more
right-priority
than
P
. This notion of right-priority implies an order on extremal paths. Moreover, since we consider a finite terminal assembly, there is a finite number of extremal paths. Thus, it is possible to choose a
rightmost-priority
one. For such a path, it is not possible to be in a case like in Figure
4
(c) where there is an intersection between
P_{s^{\prime}+i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
and
Q_{i+1,\ldots,|Q|-1}
.

3.5
Shield in the directed case with a finite terminal assembly

Our aim is to work in a setting where only the first case of Fact
3
is possible. To achieve this goal, we consider an extremal path
P
, two glue columns
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c<c^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
, and we let
(s,n)
be the span of
P
on glue column
c
and
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
the span of
P
on glue column
c^{\prime}
, for an example see Figure
5
.

c
P_{s}
P_{n}
c^{\prime}
P_{s^{\prime}}
P_{n^{\prime}}

Figure 5:
The path
P
of Figure
1
. We consider two upward spans
(s,n)
and
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
of
P
on columns
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c<c^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
respectively. Both spans point east and we suppose that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{s}P_{s+1})=\mathrm{glue}(P_{s^{\prime}}P_{s^{\prime}+1})
.

To eliminate the second case of Fact
3
where
P_{s^{\prime}}(P_{s+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}})
turns right of
P_{s^{\prime},\ldots,|P|-1}
, we consider that
P
is not an arbitrary chosen path but that
P
is chosen wisely to have the following property.

Definition 1
.

Consider an extremal path
P
, a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
and the span
(s,n)
of
P
on glue column
c
. If
(s,n)
is an upward (resp. downward) span then consider the set of extremal paths
\mathcal{P}
such that a path
Q
belongs to
\mathcal{P}
if and only if
P_{0,\ldots,s+1}
is a prefix of
Q
and
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{s}Q_{s+1})
is visible from the south (resp. north) in
Q
. Then
P
is a
good
path for glue column
c
if and only if it is the rightmost (resp. leftmost) priority path of
\mathcal{P}
.

After the roadmap, we will use a slightly weaker version of this property and we will consider
canonical
paths instead of good paths. Nevertheless, we do not enter into this level of details yet. In particular, we explain how to obtain a good/canonical path for a given glue column
c
only in Section
4
.

Lock 3
.

When the span
(s,n)
of a path
P
on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
is considered, the path
P
must be a good/canonical path for glue column
c
.

For the moment, assume that the extremal path
P
is a good path for glue column
c^{\prime}
. If the second case of Fact
3
occurs,
P_{s^{\prime}}(P_{s+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}})
turns right of
P_{s^{\prime},\ldots,|P|-1}
and a contradiction can be found. Indeed, let
Q=P_{s+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}
and suppose that
P_{0,\ldots,s^{\prime}}Q
is a producible path. Since
c<c^{\prime}
then
x_{P_{s}}<x_{P_{s^{\prime}}}
and
e_{Q}>e_{P}=e_{\gamma}
. This is a contradiction with the fact that
P
is extremal, since
P_{0,\dots,s^{\prime}}Q
goes further east than
P
by
c^{\prime}-c
columns. Then by Fact
2
,
Q
must leave the workspace
\mathcal{D}
of the span
(s^{\prime},n^{\prime})
of
P
but if
Q
turns right of
P_{s^{\prime},\ldots,|P|-1}
, all cases lead to contradictions (see Figure
6
):

•

if
Q
crosses
l^{s^{\prime}}
: then
Q-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}=P_{s+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
intersects
l^{s^{\prime}}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}=l^{s}
contradicting the fact that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{s}P_{s+1})
is visible from the south;

•

if
Q
intersects
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
: then either
P
intersects itself or
P
is not a good path for glue column
c^{\prime}
because a more right-priority path exists (by a reasoning similar to the one of Figure
4
(c));

•

if
Q
crosses
l^{n^{\prime}}
: then to reach this ray requires either to cross
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
which is the previous case or to put a tile on column
x_{|P|-1}+1=e_{\gamma}+1
which contradicts that
P
is extremal.

Then the second case of Fact
3
cannot occur if
P
is a good path for column
c^{\prime}
, meaning that only the first case is possible, in which
P_{s^{\prime},\dots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}
must turn right of
P_{s,\ldots,n+1}
.
Similarly, assume that
P
is also a good path for glue column
c
and consider the workspace
\mathcal{C}
of the span
(s,n)
of
P
(see Figure
7
). This workspace can be cut in two areas
\mathcal{C}^{+}
and
\mathcal{C}^{-}
by the ray starting at
\mathrm{pos}(P_{|P|-1})
and going south. The path
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}
can be written as
CS
where
C
is the largest common prefix between
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}
and
P_{s+1,\ldots,n}
, and S is the rest of the path
P_{s^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{s}P_{s^{\prime}}}
. The path
S
is a called a
shield
of span
(s,n)
and by a similar reasoning
S
is inside the workspace
\mathcal{C}^{+}
and does not insect with
P
. Indeed, the path
S
cannot intersect with
P
(since
P
is simple and a good path for glue column
c
), cannot reach column
x_{P_{|P|-1}}+1
(since
P
is extremal) or cross
l^{s}
(since
P_{s^{\prime},\ldots,|P|-1}
cannot intersect
l^{s^{\prime}}
). Note that the exact definition of a shield is more involved and is given in Section
7
. Nevertheless, the definition of
S
as a shield is correct in the context of good paths.

and it is in

.

3.6
Pseudo-visibility

We consider the span
(s,n)
of a path
P
on a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
. In this subsection, we explain how a shield
S
of this span can be used to find a
protected
and pseudo-visible glue on glue column
c
, and we list the assumptions done to obtain this result. Such a glue will have properties similar to those of a visible glue. Then, when Fact
3
is used on two spans with the same characteristics, either a shield or a contradiction is obtained. If a shield is obtained then a new protected and pseudo-visible glue can be found. This glue behaves as a visible glue and will replace the one consumed by using Fact
3
. We start by defining a pseudo-visible glue, see Figure
8
.

Definition 2
.

Consider an extremal path
P
, a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
and an index
0\leq d\leq|P|-1
. We say that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
is pseudo-visible from the south (resp. north) if and only if
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
is the southernmost (resp. northernmost) glue of
P_{d,\ldots,|P|-1}
on glue column
c
and points east.

P_{s}
P_{n}
P_{d}
S_{i}
c

We consider the path
P
of the Figure

and its shield
S
(in cyan). Here,
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
is the southernmost glue of
P_{d,\ldots,|P|-1}
on glue column
c
, and it is pseudo-visible from the south in
P
. Remark that
\mathrm{glue}(S_{i}S_{i+1})
is on column
c
and the path
P
does not intersect the segment between
\mathrm{glue}(S_{i}S_{i+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
. In this case, the shield
S
protects
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
.

Consider
s\leq d\leq u\leq|P|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
is pseudo-visible from the south (resp. north) on glue column
c
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{u}P_{u+1})
is visible from the north (resp. south) in
P_{d,\ldots,|P|+1}
on glue column

then
(u,d)
is an upward (resp. downward)

. Consider, the workspace
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
associated to the cut
(d,u)
of

. The pseudo-span
(d,u)
is

if and only if, for any path
Q
such that
Q
is in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
Q_{0}
interacts with
P_{d}
,
P_{0,\ldots,d}Q
is producible. Note that any span is a visible pseudo-span but a pseudo-span is not necessarily visible, see Figures
9
and
10
. Intuitively, a visible pseudo-span behaves like a span. Shields will be used to find new visible pseudo-spans (in particular those which are not spans).

, we represent here the pseudo-span

such that

binds with

The proof of the main Theorem
1
is done by showing that the larger the path is, the more constrained it is. The first proven bound, in
[
17
]
, uses only two glues per glue column: the glue visible from the north and the glue visible from the south. When the proof requires more glues to find a contradiction, a new glue column is considered. Here, shields will be used to locate visible pseudo-span, allowing to work with more than two glues per glue column. Now, we explain how to locate a visible pseudo-span on a simple case where several hypotheses on the shield are assumed. These hypotheses will allow a shield to
protect
a glue of
P
. The general case is far more complex and these locks will be solved later.

Let
S
be a shield of the span
(s,n)
of
P
and consider
s<d\leq|P|-1
of
P
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
is on glue column
c
and pointing east. If there exists
0\leq i\leq|S|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(S_{i}S_{i+1})
is on glue column
c
and no glue of
P
is on glue column
c
between
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(S_{i}S_{i+1})
, then
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
is
protected
by shield
S
, see Figure
8
. Note that, we propose here a simplified definition of a protected glue which is sufficient for the roadmap. The formal definition will be given in Section
7
. First, remark that shield
S
must intersect glue column
c
to be useful according to this definition.

Lock 4
.

Consider an extremal path
P
such that
P
is a good path on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
. Let
S
be a shield of the span
(s,n)
of
P
on glue column
c
. Then, a glue of
S
is on glue column
c
.

We explain in Section
7
how to find a protected glue when a shield satisfies the hypothesis of Lock
4
. In this roadmap, we assume that this protected glue is pseudo-visible.

Lock 5
.

Consider an extremal path
P
and a span
(s,n)
like in Lock
4
. There exists
s\leq d\leq|P|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
is pseudo-visible and protected by shield
S
.

Moreover, we also add this condition to avoid complex cases which are discussed later.

Lock 6
.

Consider an extremal path
P
, a span
(s,n)
as in Lock 4 and an index
s\leq d\leq|P|-1
as in Lock 5. Then
d\leq n
.

It follows from Locks

and
6
that
(d,n)
is a pseudo-span. According to Lock

, shield
S
protects
\mathrm{glue}(P_{d}P_{d+1})
. These hypotheses are sufficient to show that the pseudo-span
(s,n)
is visible.

Lock 7
.

Consider an extremal path
P
with a span
(s,n)
like in Lock 4, and an index
s\leq d\leq|P|-1
satisfying the property in Lock 6. Then the pseudo-span
(d,n)
of

is visible.

To obtain this result, consider a path
Q
such that

interacts with

and

is inside the workspace
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of the pseudo-span
(d,n)
of

as shown in Figures

and

. In this example,

is not a path since the last tile of

. Note furthermore that

(Figure

). Then, by merging parts of

,

, which contradicts the assumption that

is a good path for glue column

.

Another way for

is to avoid

by turning around it. To do so,

must grow to the east and then go back to the west, see Figure

. This pattern is called a

and a key point of the proof of the pumping lemma
[
17
]
is to prove that there is a contradiction if the U-turn is long enough. A quadratic bound was proven in
[

]
. To obtain the main result of this work, we must improve this bound to a linear one. Solving Lock

is done in two steps, see Figure

for an illustration.

Lock 9
.

Consider a path
Q
inside the workspace
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
of the pseudo-span
(d,n)
of

, such that
Q_{0}

(whose value depends on glue column

\mathrm{glue}(Q_{j}Q_{j+1})

\mathcal{B}_{3}
.

Informally, these two locks capture the intuition that shield
S
is an obstacle preventing any collision between
Q
and
P_{0,\dots,d}
, and ensures that pseudo-span
(d,n)
is visible.

If all these arguments hold then we can consider
2|T|+1
spans of
P
which are located on different glue columns between
\mathcal{B}_{1}=e_{\sigma+|T|+1.5}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}=e_{\sigma+5|T|+1.5}
. By the pigeonhole principle, two of these spans have the same direction and type. According to the reasoning presented so far, either there is a contradiction (
P
is not extremal or
P
is not a good path for a given column) or a shield can be assembled which can be used to find a visible pseudo-span. In the considered collection of
2|T|+1
spans, one of them can be replaced by this new visible pseudo-span. Note that the new pseudo-span is narrower than the replaced one. By iterating this reasoning, we eventually find a contradiction.

3.7
Summary of the main obstacles

To solve the different locks, we start by explaining how to build a canonical path for a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
in Section
4
, solving Lock
3
. A canonical path is a slightly modified good path (see Definition
1
). These modifications are done to deal with Lock
6
. In Section
5
, we give a linear bound on the length of U-turns, solving Locks
8
and
9
(and thus Lock
7
). Alongside the tools developed in this section, we remind the arguments of
[
18
]
to solve Lock
1
and the arguments of
[
17
]
to solve Lock
2
. Thirdly, we introduce in Section
6
the decomposition of a canonical path
P
on glue column
c
into arcs
5
5

5

This decomposition was first introduced in
[
18
]
but was later abandoned in the final version of the paper after some simplifications.
to solve Lock
5
. This decomposition allows to find protected glues which are not pseudo-visible. In Section
7
, we give the formal definition of a shield and put all arguments together to prove the main Theorem
1
by following the reasoning of this roadmap. Finally, we describe how to deal with the special case of Lock
4
.

4
Canonical paths

In the roadmap, the reasoning relies on good paths (see Definition
1
) but we stated that canonical paths are required in the main proof. These canonical paths are obtained by modifying a good path. Then, we start by explaining how to find good paths (Lemma
3
) to solve Lock
3
. Afterwards, we give the definition of canonical path (Definition
4
) and how to find them (Lemma
4
).

Lemma 3
.

Consider a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
, there exists a good path
P
for glue column
c
.

Proof.

Consider
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
, the set
\mathcal{Q}
of extremal paths and let

w=\min\{\text{width of the span of $Q$ on glue column $c$}:Q\in\mathcal{Q}\}.

We consider
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
such that the span
(s,n)
of
Q
on glue column
c
is of width
w
(note that several paths of
\mathcal{Q}
may be of width
w
, any of them can be chosen as
Q
. Depending of this choice different good paths for column
c
can be found) and suppose that this span is an upward (resp. downward) span. Let
P
be the rightmost (resp. leftmost) priority extremal path such
Q_{0,\ldots,s+1}
is a prefix of
P
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{s}P_{s+1})
is visible from the south (resp. north) in
P
. We claim that
P
is a good path for glue column
c
.

Solving Lock
3
requires only one last remark. In the example of the roadmap (Section
3
), we considered one path which was a good path for two glue columns
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c<c^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}

and another good path for glue column

. Thus, in the final Section

but we will consider a set of
4|T|+1

\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

Now, the problem of Lock
6
is similar to the one occurring in the proof of Lemma
3
and Figure

. Doing some modification on a path may switch the direction of a span. Indeed, consider a glue column

(n,d)

(n,d)
is a downward span and

Definition 3
.

Consider an extremal path
P
, a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
. The decomposition of
P
into pseudo-visible glues on glue column
c
is the sequence
(u_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t}
such that:

•

(u_{0},u_{1})
is the span of
P
on column
c
;

•

for
1\leq i<t
,
\mathrm{glue}(P_{u_{i+1}}P_{u_{i+1}+1})
is the next pseudo-visible glue of the pseudo-span
(u_{i-1},u_{i})
of
P
;

•

the sequence ends with
u_{t}=\ell
where
\ell
is the index of the last glue of
P
on glue column
c
.

Intuitively, if
(s,n)
is the span of glue column
c
of an extremal
P
and
\ell
is the index of the last glue of
P
on column
c
then the subpath
P_{s,\ldots,\ell}
of
P
is decomposed into a sequence of pseudo-spans
(u_{i},u_{i+1})_{0\leq i<t}
, see Figure
15
. Consider one of these pseudo-spans
(u_{i},u_{i+1})
then it follows from the definition of the decomposition that the direction of
(u_{i},u_{i+1})
is the inverse of the previous pseudo-span
(u_{i-1},u_{i})
. Also, consider the workspace
\mathcal{C}
of the pseudo-span
(u_{i},u_{i+1})
, then
P_{u_{i+1}+1,\ldots|P|-1}
does not leave this workspace, see Figure
16
. This remark implies the following properties
1
and
2
. First, since
P_{u_{i+1}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
is in the east area of this cut then the westernmost tile of
P_{u_{i},\ldots,|P|-1}
belongs to
P_{u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+1}}
.

d
\ell
n
\mathcal{C}

, the workspace
\mathcal{C}

w_{P_{n,\ldots,d}}<w_{P_{d+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}

.

Property 1
.

Consider an extremal path
P
, a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
and its decomposition
(u_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t}
into pseudo-visible glues on glue column
c
. Then, for any
0\leq i<t
, we have
w_{P_{u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+1}}}<w_{P_{u_{i+1}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}
.

Secondly, if
(u_{i},u_{i+1})
is an upward (resp. downward) span then
y_{P_{u_{i}}}<y_{P_{u_{i+2}}}<y_{P_{u_{i+1}}}
(resp.
y_{P_{u_{i+1}}}<y_{P_{u_{i+2}}}<y_{P_{u_{i}}}
).
This means that if
\mathrm{glue}(P_{u_{0}}P_{u_{0}+1})
is visible from the south then
(u_{2i})_{0\leq i\leq\lfloor t/2\rfloor}
and
(u_{2i+1})_{0\leq i\leq\lfloor t/2\rfloor}
are increasing and decreasing sequences respectively
6
6

6

If
\mathrm{glue}(P_{u_{0}}P_{u_{0}+1})
is visible from the north then
(u_{2i})_{0\leq i\leq\lfloor t/2\rfloor}
and
(u_{2i+1})_{0\leq i\leq\lfloor t/2\rfloor}
are decreasing and increasing sequences respectively. In both cases, these two sequences are bounded by
y_{P_{\ell}}
.
, implying that:

Property 2
.

Consider an extremal path
P
, a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
and its decomposition
(u_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t}
into pseudo-visible glues on glue column
c
. Then the widths of the pseudo-spans
(u_{i},u_{i+1})_{0\leq i<t}
are strictly decreasing.

The definition of a good path (Definition
1
) links the direction of the span and the priority of the end of the path. Now we want a similar property for each pseudo-span of the decomposition, see Figure
15
.

Definition 4
.

Consider an extremal path
P
, a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
and the decomposition
(u_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t}
of
P
into pseudo-visible glues on glue column
c
. The path
P
is
canonical
for glue column
c
if and only if for all
0\leq i<t
, if
(u_{i},u_{i+1})
is an upward (resp. downward) span then
P_{0,\ldots,u_{i+1}}
is a prefix of the rightmost (resp. leftmost) priority extremal path with
P_{0,\ldots,u_{i}+1}
as a prefix and with
\mathrm{glue}(P_{u_{i}}P_{u_{i}+1})
visible from the south (resp. north).

With this definition, if we need to consider a new pseudo-span in the case of Lock
6
, then the direction of this pseudo-span and the priority of
P
on this subpath will match. To finish, this section, we show that canonical paths exist.

Lemma 4
.

Consider a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
, there exists a canonical path
P
for glue column
c
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3
, there is a good path
P
for glue column
c
. To create a canonical path, we proceed similarly as in the proof of Lemma
3
for each pseudo-span
(u_{i},u_{i+1})
generated by the decomposition
(u_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t}
of
P
into pseudo-visible glues on column
c
.

More formally, if
P
is a canonical path for column
c
then the proof ends here. Otherwise, let
1\leq j<t
be the smallest integer such that if
(u_{j},u_{j+1})
is an upward (resp. downward) pseudo-span then
P_{0,\ldots,u_{j+1}}
is not a prefix of the rightmost (resp. leftmost) priority extremal path with
P_{0,\ldots,u_{j}+1}
as a prefix and with
\mathrm{glue}(P_{u_{j}}P_{u_{j}+1})
visible from the south (resp. north). Then, consider the rightmost (resp. leftmost) priority extremal path
Q
such that
P_{0,\ldots,u_{j}+1}
is a prefix of
Q
and
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{u_{j}}Q_{u_{j}+1})
is pseudo-visible from the south (resp. north). From now on, we suppose that
(u_{j},u_{j+1})
is an upward span (the other case is symmetric). Consider the decomposition
(u^{\prime}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t^{\prime}}
of
Q
into pseudo-visible glues on glue column
c
. The next step is to prove that
u_{i}=u^{\prime}_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq j
. To do so, since
P_{0,\ldots,u_{j}+1}
is a prefix of
Q
, we must prove that all glues of
Q_{j+1,\ldots,|Q|-1}
which are on glue column
c
have a
y
-axis between
y_{P_{u_{j}}}
and
y_{P_{u_{j+1}}}
. Consider the area
\mathcal{C}^{+}
delimited by the visibility ray of
\mathrm{glue}(P_{u_{j}}P_{u_{j}+1})
,
P_{u_{j},\ldots,|P|-1}
and the ray starting at
\mathrm{pos}(|P|)-1
and going south, see Figure
17
. If
Q_{u_{j}+1,\ldots,|Q|-1}
is inside this area then our claim holds. By definition,
Q_{u_{j}+1,\ldots,|Q|-1}
cannot leave this area by crossing through
P_{u_{j}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
or the visibility ray of
\mathrm{glue}(P_{u_{j}}P_{u_{j}+1})
and it cannot reach column
e_{P}+1=e_{\gamma}+1
. Now, we continue the same reasoning on path
Q
: either
Q
is canonical for column
c
or there is an index
j^{\prime}>j
such that the required property is not satisfied. Eventually, we found a canonical path after a number of steps bounded by the width of
(u_{0},u_{1})
(since the widths of the pseudo-spans are strictly decreasing by property
2
).

d
l^{n}
n
\mathcal{C^{-}}
\mathcal{C^{+}}

.

∎

5
U-turn

In this part, we develop a toolbox to explain how shields are able to prevent different parts of the path to interact with each other (see Section
3.6
of the roadmap). To do so, we prove first a technical Lemma
5
in Subsection
5.1
. This lemma is a formal proof that only the first case of Fact
3
can occur. Note that, this lemma is also a rewriting of the Shield Lemma of
[
17
]
. The proof is far simpler here, since we are working in the directed case with a finite terminal assembly and on paths with specific properties. Thus, the statement of this lemma is different from the one of
[
17
]
but all the arguments are taken from this previous paper. Indeed, we use this result to solve Lock
2
(Lemma
6
) following the reasoning done in
[
17
]
. Similarly, we use this result to solve Lock
1
(Lemma
7
) following the unpublished reasoning done in
[
18
]
. Then, we proceed to improve the quadratic bound of
[
17
]
for U-turn into a linear one to solve Lock
9
(Lemma
8
and Corollary
1
) in Subsection
5.2
. Solving Lock
8
(Lemma
11
) in Subsection
5.3
unfortunately requires to do reasonings similar to the ones in Lemma
5
and Lemma
8
. We think that there should exist more general versions of these Lemmas. Such a result would slightly simplify this proof and would be a powerful tool for further studies. Unfortunately, we were not able to obtain such a result here. Finally, we conclude this section by solving Lock
7
by putting all the arguments together in Lemma
12
. In this section only, we reason about
visible tiles
and not just visible glues.

5.1
The U-turn lemma and its corollaries

Lemma 5
.

Consider a producible path
P
such that its last tile is visible from the north, we denote by
l^{P}
the ray starting at
\mathrm{pos}(P_{|P|-1})
and going north. Suppose that there exist
0\leq i\leq j\leq|P|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{j}P_{j+1})
are both pointing east, have the same type and are visible from the south on glue columns
c
and
c^{\prime}
respectively. Also, assume that
P
is the rightmost-priority path with
P_{0,\ldots,i+1}
as a prefix, with
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
visible from the south and with its last tile on tile ray
l^{P}
. Then, we have:

w_{P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}\leq w_{P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}+(c-c^{\prime}).

Proof.

Consider the workspace
\mathcal{C}
which is the right side of the cut done by
l^{i}
,
l^{P}
and
P_{i,\ldots,|P|-1}
. By Lemma
2
, we have
c\leq c^{\prime}
and then the workspace
\mathcal{C}
is cut into two parts
\mathcal{C^{-}}
and
\mathcal{C^{+}}
by the glue ray
l^{j}
as shown in Figure
18
(a). Remark that if
(P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}})
is inside
\mathcal{C}
then
P_{0,\ldots,i}(P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}})
is producible. In this case, we must have
w_{P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}\leq w_{P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}+(c-c^{\prime})
in order to have enough space inside
\mathcal{C}
for
(P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}})
to grow inside it, see Figure
18
(b).

Indeed, if

(since

), see Figure

(b). Thus,

is inside
\mathcal{C}
and the result is true.

For the sake of contradiction, the last remaining case is
P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
turning right of
P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
, see Figure
19
. By the same arguments,
P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
is inside
\mathcal{C}^{-}
since this path cannot intersect
l^{j}
(since
P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
does not intersect with
l^{i}
due to the visibility of
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
) and
P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
or
l^{P}
(because of the priority of
P
). Then
P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}+\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
is inside
\mathcal{C}^{-}

(and in particular,
P_{i+1,\ldots,j}+\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
is inside
\mathcal{C}^{-}
). By definition
P_{i+1,\ldots,j}
is inside
\mathcal{C}^{+}
. Then
P_{i+1,\ldots,j}
and
P_{i+1,\ldots,j}+\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
do not intersect. By a folklore lemma (see
[
17
]
, for example),
Q=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}(P_{i+1,\ldots,j}+k\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}})
is an infinite self-avoiding path. If

is inside
\mathcal{C}
then
P_{0,\ldots,i}Q
is producible which contradicts the fact that the terminal assembly
\gamma
is finite. Then, the path
Q
must leave
\mathcal{C}
. Nevertheless,
Q
cannot cross trough
P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
and
l^{P}
(due to the priority of
P
) and if it crosses

then consider the smallest

such that

crosses
l^{j}
. If

then
P_{i+1,\ldots,j+1}

l^{i}

\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
is visible in
P
) otherwise
P_{i+1,\ldots,j+1}+(k-1)\overrightarrow{P_{i}P_{j}}
intersects
l^{i}
. Nevertheless, reaching
\mathcal{C}^{+}
from
\mathcal{C}^{-}
requires to cross
l^{j}
which contradicts the definition of
k
.

When the hypotheses of Lemma
5
are met, a path must create an area near its beginning in order to allow a copy of its end to grow inside this area. In fact this lemma will be used to show that U-turn are impossible because there is not enough space to create this area, see Lemma
8
. We illustrate how to use Lemma
5
in a simple example to solve Lock
2
(see Lemma 4.2 of
[
17
]
). Also note that there exists variant of Lemma
5
when the glues are visible from the north or when the last tile of
P
is visible from the south.

Lemma 6
.

Consider a producible path
P
whose last tile is the easternmost one and
0\leq i\leq|P|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
is visible from the south on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, then
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
points east.

Proof.

For the sake of contradiction, suppose that there exists a path
P
such that its last tile is the easternmost one and there exists
0\leq i\leq|P|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
is visible from the south on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and points west, see Figure
20
. Then consider the path
Q
such that
Q
is the leftmost priority path such that
P_{0,\ldots,i+1}
is a prefix of
Q
,
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{i}Q_{i+1})
is visible from the south (and thus points west) and
Q_{|Q|-1}
is the easternmost tile of
Q
and is on column
e_{P}
. Then by the pigeonhole principle, there exist
0\leq j\leq|Q|-1
and
0\leq k\leq|Q|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{j}Q_{j+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{k}Q_{k+1})
have the same type and they both are visible from the south on glue columns
c^{\prime}
and
c
respectively with
e_{\sigma}+0.5<c<c^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{B}_{1}
. Since
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{i}Q_{i+1})
points west then both of these glues points west by Lemma
1
, and we have
i\leq j<k
by Lemma
2
. Then we can apply Lemma
5
on
Q
with the indices
j
and
k
to obtain
e_{Q_{j,\ldots,|Q|-1}}\geq e_{Q_{k,\ldots,|Q|-1}}+(c^{\prime}-c)
. Since
e_{Q}=x_{Q_{|Q|-1}}
and
c^{\prime}>c
, there is a contradiction since the previous equation implies
e_{Q_{j,\ldots,|Q|-1}}>e_{Q}
.

Lock
1
is solved by the following result where Lemma

is applied two times.

Lemma 7
.

If
P
is an extremal path, we have
w_{P}\geq w_{\sigma}-2|T|-1
.

. Now consider

and

such that

and
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{n}Q_{n+1})
are visible from the south and from the north respectively in
Q
on glue column
w_{\sigma}-1.5-|T|
. With the same reasoning as the one of proof of Lemma

, both of these glue points east, see Figure

(a). Now consider

and let

be the shortest prefix of
P
with
Q
as a prefix and whose last tile is the easternmost one. In this case, if
y_{R_{|Q|}}=y_{R_{|Q|-1}}+1
(resp.
y_{R_{|Q|}}=y_{R_{|Q|-1}}-1
) then
\mathrm{glue}(R_{s}R_{s+1})
(resp.
\mathrm{glue}(R_{n}R_{n+1})
) is visible from the south (resp. north) in
R
and is still pointing east. Again, we obtain a contradiction with the same reasoning, see Figure

(b).

5.2
Linear bound for U-turn and values of bounds
\mathcal{B}_{3}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}

In order to explain the properties of a shield, we give the definition of the bounds
\mathcal{B}_{3}
and
\mathcal{B}_{4}
. Since these bounds will be used to analyze an extremal path which is canonical for a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
, these two bounds depends of the value of
c
. Then, we explain how to improve the quadratic bound of
[
17
]
for U-turn into a linear one.

Definition 5
.

Consider a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
, we define the functions:

\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)=2c-w_{\sigma}+3|T|+2

and

\mathcal{B}_{4}(c)=c+3|\sigma|+24|T|+14

Fact 4
.

Note that
\mathcal{B}_{5}
is defined such that the maximum of
\mathcal{B}_{4}
is reached for

\mathcal{B}_{4}(\mathcal{B}_{2})=\mathcal{B}_{5}-0.5

Also, for any
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq c^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
, we have
\mathcal{B}_{4}(c)=\mathcal{B}_{4}(c^{\prime})+(c^{\prime}-c)
.

In the following result, we consider an area delimited by a visible glue and a visible tile. We supposed that the path does a U-turn between this glue and this tile: it grows far to the east before coming back to the west. Then, Lemma
5
is applied
|T|
times to bring this U-turn close to the beginning the area.

Lemma 8
.

Consider a producible path
P
, a glue column
c_{i}
and a column
c
. Suppose that there exists a
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
which is visible from the south (resp. north) and pointing east on glue column
c_{i}
and that the last tile of
P
is visible from the north (resp. south) on column
c
. Moreover,
P
is the rightmost (resp. leftmost) priority path with
P_{0,\ldots,i+1}
as a prefix, such that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
is visible from the south (resp. north) and with its last tile visible from the north (resp. south) on column
c
. Then, we have

e_{P}-c\leq(c_{i}-w_{P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}})+|T|+0.5.

Proof.

Consider
P
,
0\leq i\leq|P|-1
,
c_{i}
and
c
which satisfies the hypothesis of the lemma, see Figure

(a). Consider

. Let

and is the westernmost glue visible from the south in
P
on glue column
c_{1}
with
c\leq c_{1}\leq e_{P}-0.5
, see Figure

(b). By Lemmas
1
and
2
, we have
i\leq j\leq k
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{j}P_{j+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{k}P_{k+1})
are both pointing east. Then Lemma

can be applied on indices
j
and

(see Figure

) to obtain

\displaystyle w_{P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}

\displaystyle\leq w_{P_{k+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}+(c_{1}-(e_{P}-0.5))

(1)

\displaystyle\leq c+(c_{1}-(e_{P}-0.5))

(2)

, see Figures

(b) and
24
. By Lemmas
1
and
2
, we have
i\leq j^{\prime}\leq k^{\prime}<j
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{j^{\prime}}P_{j^{\prime}+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(P_{k^{\prime}}P_{k^{\prime}+1})
are both pointing east. Then Lemma
5
can be applied again on indices
j^{\prime}
and
k^{\prime}
to obtain:

\displaystyle w_{P_{j^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}

\displaystyle\leq w_{P_{k^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}+(c_{2}-(c_{1}-1))

\displaystyle\leq w_{P_{j+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}+(c_{2}-(c_{1}-1))\text{ (since $k^{\prime}<j^{\prime}$)}

\displaystyle\leq c+(c_{1}-e_{P}+0.5)+(c_{2}-(c_{1}-1))\text{ (by equation (2))}

\displaystyle\leq c+(c_{2}-e_{P}+1.5).

Figure 24:
Proof of Lemma
8
, Part(3/3) (a) By Lemma
5
,
P_{k^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{j^{\prime}}P_{k^{\prime}}}
(in orange and red) is a producible path and is inside the area
\mathcal{C}_{2}
in yellow. (b) This result implies that, after
\mathrm{glue}(P_{j^{\prime}}P_{j^{\prime}+1})
the path
P
(this suffix of
P
is in light green) must do a U-turn of at least the same size than the one of after
\mathrm{glue}(P_{k^{\prime}}P_{k^{\prime}+1})
(5.5 tiles in this example) to allow
P_{k^{\prime}+1,\ldots,|P|-1}-\overrightarrow{P_{j^{\prime}}P_{k^{\prime}}}
to assemble. Then, we keep going on with the same reasoning for each tile type. Eventually, we obtain that after
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
which is on glue column
c_{i}
, the path
P
does a U-turn of at least the same size than the one after
\mathrm{glue}(P_{k}P_{k+1})
minus
|T|
.

This reasoning can be done at most
|T|
time (since each tile type is considered at most one time) until it is applied on the type of the
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
, then we obtain:

w_{P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}\leq c+(c_{i}-e_{P}+0.5+|T|).

∎

Finally, solving Lock
9
requires only to add that
w_{P}
is bounded by
w_{\sigma}-2|T|-1
by Lemma
7
.

Corollary 1
.

Consider a producible path
P
, a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
. Suppose that there exists a
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
which is visible from the south (resp. north) in
P
and pointing east on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)+1.5
. If the last tile of
P
is visible from the north (resp. south) on column
\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)+1
then
e_{P}<\mathcal{B}_{4}(c)
.

Proof.

Consider a producible path
P
, a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
. Suppose that there exists a
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
which is visible from the south in
P
(the other case is symmetric) and pointing east on glue column
\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)+1.5
. If the last tile of
P
is visible from the north on column
\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)+1
then, by Lemma
8
, we have

e_{P}-(\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)+1)\leq(\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)+1.5-w_{P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}})+|T|+0.5

Thus,

w_{P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}}\leq 2\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)-e_{P}+|T|+3

By Lemma
7
, we have
w_{P}\geq w_{\sigma}-2|T|-1
which leads to:

w_{\sigma}-2|T|-1\leq 2\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)-e_{P}+|T|+3

And then,

\displaystyle e_{P}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)-w_{\sigma}+3|T|+4

\displaystyle\leq 4c-3w_{\sigma}+9|T|+8

\displaystyle\leq c+3(c-w_{\sigma})+9|T|+8

\displaystyle\leq c+3(|\sigma|+5|T|+1.5)+9|T|+8

\displaystyle\leq c+3|\sigma|+24|T|+12.5

\displaystyle<\mathcal{B}_{4}(c)

Note that the inequality
c-w_{\sigma}\leq 5|T|+1.5+|\sigma|
is due the fact that
c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
,
\mathcal{B}_{2}=e_{\sigma}+5|T|+1.5
and
e_{\sigma}-w_{\sigma}\leq|\sigma|

∎

5.3
U-turn for a canonical path

Consider a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
and a path
P
which is canonical for glue column
c
, the span
(s,n)
of
P
on
c
and the index of the last glue
\ell
of
P
on
c
. The objective of this subsection is to bound
e_{P_{0,\ldots,\ell}}
by
\mathcal{B}_{3}(c)
(see Lemma
11
) to solve Lock
8
. Using Lemma
8
directly and doing some calculus may allow to bound only
e_{P_{0,\ldots,n+1}}
which is not sufficient for our objective. To prove this result, we will follow the same steps as in Subsection
5.2
but we will adapt the proofs to canonical paths.

The first problem is that the last tile of
P_{0,\ldots,\ell}
may not be the easternmost one of
P_{0,\ldots,\ell}
. The second one is that a glue which is visible in
P_{0,\ldots,\ell}
may not be visible in
P
, see Figure
25
. To solve this problem, we will mainly work on the
useful prefix

Q
of the canonical path
P
which is is defined as the shortest prefix of
P
to reach column
e_{P_{0,\ldots,\ell}}+1
, see Figure
25
(b). Note that this notion is used only in this Subsection. Now, we prove some useful results on the visible glues of
Q
to obtain equivalences of Lemmas
1
and
2
.

Lemma 9
.

Consider a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
and a path
P
which is canonical for
c
. Let
Q
be the useful prefix of
P
and let
(u_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t}
be the decomposition of
P
into pseudo-visible glue on glue column
c
. Then, there exists
u_{0}\leq k\leq u_{t}
such that
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{k}Q_{k+1})
is visible from either the south or north on glue column
e_{Q}-1.5
. If
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{k}Q_{k+1})
is visible from the south (resp. north), then consider
c\leq c_{i}\leq e_{Q}-1.5
and
0\leq i\leq|Q|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{i}Q_{i+1})
is visible from the south (resp. north) in
Q
on glue column
c_{i}
, then
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{i}Q_{i+1})
points east and there exists
0\leq i^{\prime}<t
such that
u_{i^{\prime}}\leq i<u_{i^{\prime}+1}
and
(u_{i^{\prime}},u_{i^{\prime}+1})
is an upward (resp. downward) pseudo-span of
Q
on glue column
c
. Moreover, if
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{j}Q_{j+1})
is visible from the south (resp. north) in
Q
on a glue column
c_{i}\leq c_{j}\leq e_{Q}-1.5
, then we have
i\leq j
.

Proof.

Let
R
be the shortest prefix of
Q
to reach column
e_{P_{0,\ldots,u_{t}+1}}
(in orange in Figure
25
). Since
(u_{0},u_{1})
is the span of
Q
on column
c
we have
e_{P_{0,\ldots,u_{0}-1}}<e_{P_{u_{0},\ldots,u_{1}}}
. Then,
u_{0}\leq|R|-1
and
\mathrm{glue}(R_{u_{0}}R_{u_{0}+1})
is a visible glue of
R
. Without loss of generality, we suppose that
\mathrm{glue}(R_{u_{0}}R_{u_{0}+1})
is a visible glue from the north (
i.e.

(u_{0},u_{1})
is a downward span) and
y_{Q_{|R|}}=y_{Q_{|R|-1}}+1
(the other cases are symmetric) and we call
l^{R}
the ray starting at
\mathrm{pos}(R_{|R|-1})
and going east. Let
\mathcal{C}
be the north-east side of the cut delimited by
l^{u_{0}}
,
R_{u_{0},\ldots,|R|-1}
and
l^{R}
, see Figure
25
(b).

Remark that the tile
Q_{|R|}
is inside
\mathcal{C}
since
y_{Q_{|R|}}=y_{Q_{|R|-1}}+1
. Moreover,
Q_{|R|,\ldots,|Q|-2}
cannot cross
l^{R}
(it cannot reach column
e_{R}+1
),
R_{u_{0},\ldots,|R|-1}
(since
P
is simple) and
l^{u_{0}}
(since
\mathrm{glue}(R_{u_{0}}R_{u_{0}+1})
is visible in
P
). Finally since
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{|Q|-2}Q_{|Q|-1})
is on column
e_{R}+0.5
then
Q_{|R|,\ldots,|Q|-2}
is inside
\mathcal{C}
. Thus, consider glue column
c\leq c_{i}\leq e_{Q}-1.5
and
0\leq i\leq|R|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(R_{i}R_{i+1})
is visible from the south in
R
, since
l^{i}
is not inside
\mathcal{C}
(apart from its starting point) then
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{i}Q_{i+1})
is visible from the south in
Q
. Since the last tile of
Q
is the easternmost one then Lemmas
1
and
2
show that
\mathrm{glue}(P_{i}P_{i+1})
point east and that
u_{0}\leq i
. Since
i\leq|R|-1\leq u_{t}
, there exists
0\leq i^{\prime}\leq t
such that
u_{i^{\prime}}\leq i<u_{i^{\prime}+1}
. If
(u_{i^{\prime}},u_{i^{\prime}+1})
is an upward pseudo-span then the lemma is true.
Otherwise, consider the east side of the cut delimited by
l^{u_{i^{\prime}}}
,
Q_{u_{i^{\prime}},\ldots,i}
and
l^{i}
, note that
l^{u_{i^{\prime}+1}}
is not inside this area and since
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{i}Q_{i+1})
points east, we obtain a contradiction, see Figure
26
.
Finally, consider a glue column
c_{i}\leq c_{j}\leq e_{Q}-1.5
and
0\leq j\leq|Q|-1
such that
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{j}Q_{j+1})
is visible from the south in
Q
on glue column
c_{j}
. Then,
i\leq j
by Lemma
2
.

. Consider the area in yellow delimited by

Lemma 10
.

Consider a glue column
\mathcal{B}_{1}\leq c\leq\mathcal{B}_{2}
and a path
P
which is canonical for
c
. Let
Q
be the useful prefix of
P
and let
(u_{i})_{0\leq i\leq t}
be the decomposition of
P
into pseudo-visible glue on column
c
. If there exist
u_{i^{\prime}}\leq i\leq u_{i^{\prime}}
and
u_{j^{\prime}}\leq j\leq u_{j^{\prime}}
such that
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{i}Q_{i+1})
and
\mathrm{glue}(Q_{j}Q_{j+1})
are visible from the south on glue column
c^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
respectively with
c\leq c^{\prime}\leq c^{\prime\prime}\leq e_{Q}-1.5
and both glues have the same type then we have:

w_{Q_{i+1,\ldots,u_{i^{\prime}+1}}}\leq w_{Q_{j+1,\ldots,u_{j^{\prime}+1}}}+(c^{\prime}-c^{\prime\prime}).

Proof.

First of all, by Lemma
9
, we have
i\leq j
and thus
i^{\prime}\leq j^{\prime}
. Consider
l^{Q}
the ray starting at
\mathrm{pos}(Q_{|Q|-1})
and going south and let
\mathcal{C}
be the area delimited by
l^{i}
,
Q_{i,\ldots,|Q|-1}
and
l^{Q}
, see Figure
27
(b). Note that
w_{Q_{i,\ldots,|Q|-1}}=w_{Q_{i,\ldots,u_{i^{\prime}+1}}}
and if
Q_{j+1,\ldots,u_{j^{\prime}+1}}-\overrightarrow{Q_{i}Q_{j}}
is inside
\mathcal{C}
then

w_{Q_{i+1,\ldots,u_{i^{\prime}+1}}}\leq w_{Q_{j+1,\ldots,u_{j^{\prime}+1}}}+(c^{\prime}-c^{\prime\prime})

and the lemma is true
7
7

7

. In this case, we cannot use the priority of
(u_{2},u_{3})
since it is in the wrong orientation. This remark explains the complexity of this proof.

Q_{i,\ldots,|Q|-1|}
and
l^{Q}
(the ray starting at

and going south). Secondly, consider

the largest prefix of
Q_{j,\ldots,u_{j^{\prime}}}-\overrightarrow{Q_{i}Q_{j}}
which is entirely inside
\mathcal{D}
(if
S=Q_{j,\ldots,u_{j^{\prime}}}-\overrightarrow{Q_{i}Q_{j}}
then we are in the previous case and the lemma is true). Let
a
such that
S_{0,\ldots,a}
is the largest prefix between
S
and
P_{i+1,\ldots,|P|-1}
. Let

such that

. If

then let

and

. In this case,

is a path and turns right of

. Otherwise, let
a<b\leq|S|-1
such that:

b=\min\{a<k:S_{k}\text{ is a tile of }P_{i,\ldots,d}\}

and let
i+a<b<d
such that
P_{b^{\prime}}=S_{b}
. By definition of

,

is a path. Remark that

and

delimit a finite area, see Figure

(a). This area cannot contains the last tile of

which is the easternmost one. Since

cannot intersect

(by definition of

) and
P_{a^{\prime},\ldots,b^{\prime}}
then

is not inside this area and turns right of
Q_{j+1,\ldots,u_{j^{\prime}+1}}-\overrightarrow{Q_{i}Q_{j}}
. Thirdly, let
A=P_{j,\ldots,g+1}
. The path

and the ray

delimit a finite area of the plane called
\mathcal{I}_{A}
(we develop this argument in the following Section

), see Figure

x_{P_{d}}+(c^{\prime\prime}-c^{\prime})>e_{P_{j,\ldots,g}}
and thus

is not inside

. Thus

must leave

(since

), cannot intersect with

(by priority of

or crosses trough

(since

is extremal). This is a contradiction.
